Um… I guess I’m here because the clinic’s been kind of on me about how little I’m eating. Yeah… I mean, I do. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.

Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.

If I actually sit down for a real meal, I don’t really relax into it, you know? It kind of feels like it throws everything off, like the whole day I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nMost days it’s easier to just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll do a yogurt or a few bites of something. Like, I know that probably sounds bad, and the clinic keeps getting on me about it, but sitting there with a full meal just feels like… too much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike at work, if they have fried stuff in the break room—somebody brings fries or chicken or whatever—I’ll be like, okay, if I eat that, is it going to show on me right away? Which I know sounds… maybe not logical. Or at church if there’s a big plate of food, people are always like, “Here, eat more,” and then I’m thinking about how I’m going to look after, not really the food.\n\nAnd then I keep thinking about it after, like I want to go check the scale and make sure nothing changed. It kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I’ve lost some. People keep saying it’s “a lot,” like my work pants are hanging off me now, and one of the women at work was making this whole thing about how I need to buy new ones or I’m gonna trip over them, which was kind of embarrassing.\n\nBut when I see the number go down, it feels like… I don’t know, like I’m finally getting somewhere. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. It just feels like they’re trying to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t always want to say it like that, because it sounds dramatic, but yeah.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or at church somebody hands me this huge plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. And then even after, I can’t really just move on, I keep thinking about it and wanting to check the scale, just to make sure nothing changed. It’s kind of always sitting there in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, even when other people say you do?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really see it that way. I know you’re worried, and people at church keep making comments too, like they’ll look at me and then look at my plate, and it turns into this whole thing.\n\nBut honestly, I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is. I’m still going to work, I’m still doing my classes, I’m keeping up with my life. Like, if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to do all that, right? So… no, I don’t really think I look too thin the way they say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a full meal would make you lose control over your body or your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.\n\nMost days I’ll just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. The clinic’s been getting on me about how little that is, which is why I’m even here, but it just feels easier that way. Like if I do a real meal, then I start thinking about what else is gonna slip, and then I’m distracted the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more like control, maybe. I mean, the weight thing is there, obviously, but if I’m being honest, most days it’s just easier to not start with food.\n\nLike before the warehouse I’ll just have coffee, and then after GED class maybe a yogurt or a couple bites of something. And then the clinic acts like that’s this huge issue, which is why I’m here, but sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up the whole day. Like then I’m thinking about it, and what I ate, and whether I should’ve eaten it, and I don’t know… it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly a straight answer. I just mean it feels safer keeping it small.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike at work, if they have fried stuff in the break room—somebody brings fries or chicken or whatever—I’ll be like, okay, if I eat that, is it going to show on me right away? Which I know sounds… maybe not logical. It kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I’ve lost some. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. People keep saying it’s “a lot,” like my work pants are hanging off me now, and one of the women at work was making this whole thing about how I need to buy new ones or I’m gonna trip over them, which was kind of embarrassing.\n\nBut when I see the number go down, it feels like… I don’t know, like I’m finally getting somewhere. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. Like, I know that probably sounds bad, and the clinic keeps getting on me about it, but sitting there with a full meal just feels like… too much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike at work, if they have fried stuff in the break room—somebody brings fries or chicken or whatever—I’ll be like, okay, if I eat that, is it going to show on me right away? Which I know sounds… maybe not logical. Or at church if there’s a big plate of food, people are always like, “Here, eat more,” and then I’m thinking about how I’m going to look after, not really the food.\n\nAnd then I keep thinking about it after, like I want to go check the scale and make sure nothing changed. It kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I’ve lost some. People keep saying it’s “a lot,” like my work pants are hanging off me now, and one of the women at work was making this whole thing about how I need to buy new ones or I’m gonna trip over them, which was kind of embarrassing.\n\nBut when I see the number go down, it feels like… I don’t know, like I’m finally getting somewhere. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. It just feels like they’re trying to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t always want to say it like that, because it sounds dramatic, but yeah.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or at church somebody hands me this huge plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. And then even after, I can’t really just move on, I keep thinking about it and wanting to check the scale, just to make sure nothing changed. It’s kind of always sitting there in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, even when other people say you do?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really see it that way. I know you’re worried, and people at church keep making comments too, like they’ll look at me and then look at my plate, and it turns into this whole thing.\n\nBut honestly, I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is. I’m still going to work, I’m still doing my classes, I’m keeping up with my life. Like, if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to do all that, right? So… no, I don’t really think I look too thin the way they say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a full meal would make you lose control over your body or your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.\n\nMost days I’ll just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. The clinic’s been getting on me about how little that is, which is why I’m even here, but it just feels easier that way. Like if I do a real meal, then I start thinking about what else is gonna slip, and then I’m distracted the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more like control, maybe. I mean, the weight thing is there, obviously, but if I’m being honest, most days it’s just easier to not start with food.\n\nLike before the warehouse I’ll just have coffee, and then after GED class maybe a yogurt or a couple bites of something. And then the clinic acts like that’s this huge issue, which is why I’m here, but sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up the whole day. Like then I’m thinking about it, and what I ate, and whether I should’ve eaten it, and I don’t know… it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly a straight answer. I just mean it feels safer keeping it small.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating a full meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Probably losing control of my routine, I guess. The weight part is in there, but the first thing is more like… if I sit down and eat a full meal, then everything gets thrown off.\n\nLike most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And I know that’s why the clinic got on me and sent me here, because they think it’s not enough, but it feels easier to keep things small. If I do a real meal, then I’m thinking about it all day, and then I’m not keeping everything in line.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I keep saying it’s about control, and it is, but gaining weight is definitely in my head too.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or if I’m at church and somebody gives me this big plate, I start thinking, “That’s gonna show on me.” Like right away, even though I know maybe that doesn’t make sense. Then I can’t stop thinking about it and I want to check the scale, just to see if something changed. So… yeah, it’s a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, most of the time it is the routine thing. Like, I know the weight fear is there, but when I’m deciding what to eat, it’s more like, “Can I keep my day together if I do this?”\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating. But most days I’m going to the warehouse, and I just grab coffee first, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like everything gets… I don’t know, loose. Like I’m not keeping it all in line anymore.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff I was supposed to do, like homework or laundry or whatever, and it just feels easier not to make food into this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about preventing weight gain or maintaining a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the control thing… like keeping my day in a certain order. I mean, the weight stuff is there too, but I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating.\n\nMost days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line. Then the whole day feels like it’s not going the way it’s supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would still restrict some. I know the weight thing matters, but it’s not only that.\n\nLike, I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating, and most days it’s just… coffee before I go to the warehouse, then maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. And if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it throws everything off, like I can’t keep the day in line anymore.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense. It’s just easier to keep moving and not make food this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control and order in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more about keeping control and order, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, but when it’s actually happening during the day, it’s more like… if I stop and eat a real meal, everything feels like it gets off track.\n\nLike I’ve said, the clinic got on me because I’m barely eating, but most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then I’ll think, okay, I still have stuff to do, and if I sit down and make food this whole thing, it feels like I’m not keeping everything lined up anymore.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going around it, but yeah… it’s more the control part.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t think it would just go away. I do worry about how I look, even when I tell myself I’m just trying to keep the day normal.

Like before class, or before softball, I’ll check in the mirror and my stomach and thighs still look… bigger than they should, I guess. And then it kind of sets the tone for everything. Like if I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, and if I don’t, it feels like I’m failing the whole day already.

Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, I think I might eat more if I didn’t care about that stuff, but it’s hard to imagine not caring.

Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think it’s a big part of it. Like if there’s fried food in the break room, or at church if someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking, “If I eat that, it’s going to show on me,” like almost right away.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because the clinic’s been kind of on me about how little I’m eating. They keep asking, and it’s like—yeah, I know it doesn’t sound like much, but most days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse. Sometimes later, after GED class, I’ll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make a big thing of it. It’s just sitting down for a real meal feels like it messes up everything I’m trying to keep in line, if that makes sense. And my schedule’s weird anyway, with packing shifts and class and all that.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have a chance to sit down for a meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… “enjoy” is hard. If I actually sit down for a real meal, I don’t really relax into it, you know? It kind of feels like it throws everything off, like the whole day I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nMost days it’s easier to just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll do a yogurt or a few bites of something. Like, I know that probably sounds bad, and the clinic keeps getting on me about it, but sitting there with a full meal just feels like… too much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike at work, if they have fried stuff in the break room—somebody brings fries or chicken or whatever—I’ll be like, okay, if I eat that, is it going to show on me right away? Which I know sounds… maybe not logical. Or at church if there’s a big plate of food, people are always like, “Here, eat more,” and then I’m thinking about how I’m going to look after, not really the food.\n\nAnd then I keep thinking about it after, like I want to go check the scale and make sure nothing changed. It kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I’ve lost some. People keep saying it’s “a lot,” like my work pants are hanging off me now, and one of the women at work was making this whole thing about how I need to buy new ones or I’m gonna trip over them, which was kind of embarrassing.\n\nBut when I see the number go down, it feels like… I don’t know, like I’m finally getting somewhere. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. It just feels like they’re trying to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t always want to say it like that, because it sounds dramatic, but yeah.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or at church somebody hands me this huge plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. And then even after, I can’t really just move on, I keep thinking about it and wanting to check the scale, just to make sure nothing changed. It’s kind of always sitting there in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, even when other people say you do?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really see it that way. I know you’re worried, and people at church keep making comments too, like they’ll look at me and then look at my plate, and it turns into this whole thing.\n\nBut honestly, I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is. I’m still going to work, I’m still doing my classes, I’m keeping up with my life. Like, if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to do all that, right? So… no, I don’t really think I look too thin the way they say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a full meal would make you lose control over your body or your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.\n\nMost days I’ll just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. The clinic’s been getting on me about how little that is, which is why I’m even here, but it just feels easier that way. Like if I do a real meal, then I start thinking about what else is gonna slip, and then I’m distracted the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more like control, maybe. I mean, the weight thing is there, obviously, but if I’m being honest, most days it’s just easier to not start with food.\n\nLike before the warehouse I’ll just have coffee, and then after GED class maybe a yogurt or a couple bites of something. And then the clinic acts like that’s this huge issue, which is why I’m here, but sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up the whole day. Like then I’m thinking about it, and what I ate, and whether I should’ve eaten it, and I don’t know… it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly a straight answer. I just mean it feels safer keeping it small.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating a full meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Probably losing control of my routine, I guess. The weight part is in there, but the first thing is more like… if I sit down and eat a full meal, then everything gets thrown off.\n\nLike most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And I know that’s why the clinic got on me and sent me here, because they think it’s not enough, but it feels easier to keep things small. If I do a real meal, then I’m thinking about it all day, and then I’m not keeping everything in line.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I keep saying it’s about control, and it is, but gaining weight is definitely in my head too.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or if I’m at church and somebody gives me this big plate, I start thinking, “That’s gonna show on me.” Like right away, even though I know maybe that doesn’t make sense. Then I can’t stop thinking about it and I want to check the scale, just to see if something changed. So… yeah, it’s a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, most of the time it is the routine thing. Like, I know the weight fear is there, but when I’m deciding what to eat, it’s more like, “Can I keep my day together if I do this?”\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating. But most days I’m going to the warehouse, and I just grab coffee first, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like everything gets… I don’t know, loose. Like I’m not keeping it all in line anymore.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff I was supposed to do, like homework or laundry or whatever, and it just feels easier not to make food into this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about preventing weight gain or maintaining a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the control thing… like keeping my day in a certain order. I mean, the weight stuff is there too, but I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating.\n\nMost days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line. Then the whole day feels like it’s not going the way it’s supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would still restrict some. I know the weight thing matters, but it’s not only that.\n\nLike, I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating, and most days it’s just… coffee before I go to the warehouse, then maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Like, I know that probably sounds bad, and the clinic keeps getting on me about it, but sitting there with a full meal just feels like… too much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do. Not every second, I guess, but if I’m deciding, it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike at work, if they have fried stuff in the break room—somebody brings fries or chicken or whatever—I’ll be like, okay, if I eat that, is it going to show on me right away? Which I know sounds… maybe not logical. Or at church if there’s a big plate of food, people are always like, “Here, eat more,” and then I’m thinking about how I’m going to look after, not really the food.\n\nAnd then I keep thinking about it after, like I want to go check the scale and make sure nothing changed. It kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I’ve lost some. People keep saying it’s “a lot,” like my work pants are hanging off me now, and one of the women at work was making this whole thing about how I need to buy new ones or I’m gonna trip over them, which was kind of embarrassing.\n\nBut when I see the number go down, it feels like… I don’t know, like I’m finally getting somewhere. Like I’m doing something right. So when people say I look too thin or that I need to put it back on, I don’t really get why that has to be the goal. It just feels like they’re trying to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t always want to say it like that, because it sounds dramatic, but yeah.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or at church somebody hands me this huge plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. And then even after, I can’t really just move on, I keep thinking about it and wanting to check the scale, just to make sure nothing changed. It’s kind of always sitting there in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, even when other people say you do?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really see it that way. I know you’re worried, and people at church keep making comments too, like they’ll look at me and then look at my plate, and it turns into this whole thing.\n\nBut honestly, I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is. I’m still going to work, I’m still doing my classes, I’m keeping up with my life. Like, if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to do all that, right? So… no, I don’t really think I look too thin the way they say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a full meal would make you lose control over your body or your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.\n\nMost days I’ll just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. The clinic’s been getting on me about how little that is, which is why I’m even here, but it just feels easier that way. Like if I do a real meal, then I start thinking about what else is gonna slip, and then I’m distracted the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more like control, maybe. I mean, the weight thing is there, obviously, but if I’m being honest, most days it’s just easier to not start with food.\n\nLike before the warehouse I’ll just have coffee, and then after GED class maybe a yogurt or a couple bites of something. And then the clinic acts like that’s this huge issue, which is why I’m here, but sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up the whole day. Like then I’m thinking about it, and what I ate, and whether I should’ve eaten it, and I don’t know… it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly a straight answer. I just mean it feels safer keeping it small.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating a full meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Probably losing control of my routine, I guess. The weight part is in there, but the first thing is more like… if I sit down and eat a full meal, then everything gets thrown off.\n\nLike most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And I know that’s why the clinic got on me and sent me here, because they think it’s not enough, but it feels easier to keep things small. If I do a real meal, then I’m thinking about it all day, and then I’m not keeping everything in line.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I keep saying it’s about control, and it is, but gaining weight is definitely in my head too.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or if I’m at church and somebody gives me this big plate, I start thinking, “That’s gonna show on me.” Like right away, even though I know maybe that doesn’t make sense. Then I can’t stop thinking about it and I want to check the scale, just to see if something changed. So… yeah, it’s a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, most of the time it is the routine thing. Like, I know the weight fear is there, but when I’m deciding what to eat, it’s more like, “Can I keep my day together if I do this?”\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating. But most days I’m going to the warehouse, and I just grab coffee first, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like everything gets… I don’t know, loose. Like I’m not keeping it all in line anymore.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff I was supposed to do, like homework or laundry or whatever, and it just feels easier not to make food into this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about preventing weight gain or maintaining a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the control thing… like keeping my day in a certain order. I mean, the weight stuff is there too, but I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating.\n\nMost days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line. Then the whole day feels like it’s not going the way it’s supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would still restrict some. I know the weight thing matters, but it’s not only that.\n\nLike, I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating, and most days it’s just… coffee before I go to the warehouse, then maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. And if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it throws everything off, like I can’t keep the day in line anymore.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense. It’s just easier to keep moving and not make food this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control and order in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more about keeping control and order, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, but when it’s actually happening during the day, it’s more like… if I stop and eat a real meal, everything feels like it gets off track.\n\nLike I’ve said, the clinic got on me because I’m barely eating, but most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then I’ll think, okay, I still have stuff to do, and if I sit down and make food this whole thing, it feels like I’m not keeping everything lined up anymore.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going around it, but yeah… it’s more the control part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t think it would just go away. I do worry about how I look, even when I tell myself I’m just trying to keep the day normal.\n\nLike before class, or before softball, I’ll check in the mirror and my stomach and thighs still look… bigger than they should, I guess. And then it kind of sets the tone for everything. Like if I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, and if I don’t, it feels like I’m failing the whole day already.\n\nSorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, I think I might eat more if I didn’t care about that stuff, but it’s hard to imagine not caring.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it’s a big part of it. Like if there’s fried food in the break room, or at church if someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking, “If I eat that, it’s going to show on me,” like almost right away.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking about it. I’ll want to check the scale, or I’ll just keep replaying what I ate in my head. It’s stupid because I know one meal probably doesn’t work like that, but it feels like it does. So yeah, the fear of gaining weight is definitely there… even if I also tell myself it’s about keeping things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would consider yourself to be at a healthy weight if you didn't have any external influences or opinions from others?\n\nPatient: I think I would, honestly. Like… I know you and some people from church are worried, and they’ll say I look too thin or they’ll ask if I’m eating enough, but I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still going to work, I’m still doing my GED classes, I’m getting up and doing what I’m supposed to do. Like at the warehouse, nobody’s carrying my boxes for me or anything. Well, sometimes they act like I need help, which kind of annoys me because I can do my job.\n\nSo… yeah, if it was just me and not everyone commenting, I’d probably say I’m okay. Maybe not perfect, but not as bad as everybody keeps making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you want it to?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I don’t know. Sometimes I think it looks okay, but then I’ll be getting ready for class or softball and I look in the mirror, and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of messes with my whole mood. Like if I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good that day, but if I don’t, it feels like I already failed somehow. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s just where my head goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… probably, yeah. Not forever forever, maybe, but I don’t really know how to just eat “normal” and not feel like everything gets out of line.

Like, the clinic got on me because I’m not eating much, and I get why they’re saying that, but most days it’s just coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. And then by the time I’m home, sitting down for a whole real meal feels like this big thing, like it’s going to mess up what I’ve been trying to keep under control.

So… no, I don’t really think I look too thin the way they say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a full meal would make you lose control over your body or your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I think one meal literally ruins my whole life, but sitting down to a full meal feels like it throws everything off, like I’m not keeping things in line anymore.\n\nMost days I’ll just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. The clinic’s been getting on me about how little that is, which is why I’m even here, but it just feels easier that way. Like if I do a real meal, then I start thinking about what else is gonna slip, and then I’m distracted the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… more like control, maybe. I mean, the weight thing is there, obviously, but if I’m being honest, most days it’s just easier to not start with food.\n\nLike before the warehouse I’ll just have coffee, and then after GED class maybe a yogurt or a couple bites of something. And then the clinic acts like that’s this huge issue, which is why I’m here, but sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up the whole day. Like then I’m thinking about it, and what I ate, and whether I should’ve eaten it, and I don’t know… it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly a straight answer. I just mean it feels safer keeping it small.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating a full meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Probably losing control of my routine, I guess. The weight part is in there, but the first thing is more like… if I sit down and eat a full meal, then everything gets thrown off.\n\nLike most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And I know that’s why the clinic got on me and sent me here, because they think it’s not enough, but it feels easier to keep things small. If I do a real meal, then I’m thinking about it all day, and then I’m not keeping everything in line.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I keep saying it’s about control, and it is, but gaining weight is definitely in my head too.\n\nLike if there’s fried food in the break room at work, or if I’m at church and somebody gives me this big plate, I start thinking, “That’s gonna show on me.” Like right away, even though I know maybe that doesn’t make sense. Then I can’t stop thinking about it and I want to check the scale, just to see if something changed. So… yeah, it’s a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, most of the time it is the routine thing. Like, I know the weight fear is there, but when I’m deciding what to eat, it’s more like, “Can I keep my day together if I do this?”\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating. But most days I’m going to the warehouse, and I just grab coffee first, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like everything gets… I don’t know, loose. Like I’m not keeping it all in line anymore.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff I was supposed to do, like homework or laundry or whatever, and it just feels easier not to make food into this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about preventing weight gain or maintaining a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the control thing… like keeping my day in a certain order. I mean, the weight stuff is there too, but I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating.\n\nMost days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line. Then the whole day feels like it’s not going the way it’s supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably would still restrict some. I know the weight thing matters, but it’s not only that.\n\nLike, I’m here because the clinic got on me about how little I’ve been eating, and most days it’s just… coffee before I go to the warehouse, then maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. And if I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it throws everything off, like I can’t keep the day in line anymore.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense. It’s just easier to keep moving and not make food this whole big thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control and order in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more about keeping control and order, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, but when it’s actually happening during the day, it’s more like… if I stop and eat a real meal, everything feels like it gets off track.\n\nLike I’ve said, the clinic got on me because I’m barely eating, but most days I just get coffee before the warehouse, and then maybe after GED class I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something. And then I’ll think, okay, I still have stuff to do, and if I sit down and make food this whole thing, it feels like I’m not keeping everything lined up anymore.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going around it, but yeah… it’s more the control part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t think it would just go away. I do worry about how I look, even when I tell myself I’m just trying to keep the day normal.\n\nLike before class, or before softball, I’ll check in the mirror and my stomach and thighs still look… bigger than they should, I guess. And then it kind of sets the tone for everything. Like if I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, and if I don’t, it feels like I’m failing the whole day already.\n\nSorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, I think I might eat more if I didn’t care about that stuff, but it’s hard to imagine not caring.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it’s a big part of it. Like if there’s fried food in the break room, or at church if someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking, “If I eat that, it’s going to show on me,” like almost right away.\n\nAnd then I can’t stop thinking about it. I’ll want to check the scale, or I’ll just keep replaying what I ate in my head. It’s stupid because I know one meal probably doesn’t work like that, but it feels like it does. So yeah, the fear of gaining weight is definitely there… even if I also tell myself it’s about keeping things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would consider yourself to be at a healthy weight if you didn't have any external influences or opinions from others?\n\nPatient: I think I would, honestly. Like… I know you and some people from church are worried, and they’ll say I look too thin or they’ll ask if I’m eating enough, but I feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still going to work, I’m still doing my GED classes, I’m getting up and doing what I’m supposed to do. Like at the warehouse, nobody’s carrying my boxes for me or anything. Well, sometimes they act like I need help, which kind of annoys me because I can do my job.\n\nSo… yeah, if it was just me and not everyone commenting, I’d probably say I’m okay. Maybe not perfect, but not as bad as everybody keeps making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you want it to?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I don’t know. Sometimes I think it looks okay, but then I’ll be getting ready for class or softball and I look in the mirror, and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of messes with my whole mood. Like if I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good that day, but if I don’t, it feels like I already failed somehow. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s just where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern something you think you would continue even if you were at a weight that you considered ideal?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yeah. Not forever forever, maybe, but I don’t really know how to just eat “normal” and not feel like everything gets out of line.\n\nLike, the clinic got on me because I’m not eating much, and I get why they’re saying that, but most days it’s just coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. And then by the time I’m home, sitting down for a whole real meal feels like this big thing, like it’s going to mess up what I’ve been trying to keep under control.\n\nIt’s not even always about hitting some exact weight. It’s more like… once I start eating more, I feel like everything’s going to slide, and then I’ll have to fix it again. So even if I got to what I thought was the right weight, I think I’d still be careful. Or I’d try to be."
